Finally, as long as demand for drugs does not decline and Mexico does not find a more effective\nstrategy, it is hard to imagine cartels’ activity will substantially diminish after AMLO. On the contrary, the\nconsequences of AMLO’s policies, which have fueled violence, will be difficult to erase in the short term.\nHuman Security and Human Rights\nIncreased activity and territorial control by drug cartels and excessive militarization by the government have\ncreated mounting challenges for Mexican citizens, especially for vulnerable populations, including women,\nLGBTQ+, and Indigenous communities. Conflicts between the state and criminal groups or between different\ncartels often involve collateral damage and produce effects on public security, spreading fear and a sense of\ndanger. Corruption that favors the illegal trafficking of drugs and other substances permeates different levels of\nsociety, including the judicial system, resulting in a persistent climate of impunity. Insecurity also affects freedom\nof expression, limiting critiques and silencing, often brutally, activists and anti-corruption campaigners.\nMexico is one of the most dangerous countries in the world for human rights defenders and journalists, on\npar with Syria, Afghanistan, and war zones.86 In 2022 the United Nations Educational, Scientific, and Cultural\nOrganization (UNESCO) reported 19 deaths of journalists in Mexico, while the Federal Special Prosecutor’s Office\ndealt with 3,362 investigations of crimes against journalists in 2021.87 Of these investigations, only around 25 led\nto convictions.88 The Committee to Protect Journalists registered 2020 as the worst year for journalists in Mexico:\nthey received over 692 threats and attacks, and the trend line since has shown little improvement.89\nDespite commitments to gender equality and nondiscrimination after Mexico became the first country in\nLatin America to adopt a feminist foreign policy for 2020–24, women in Mexico are facing severe difficulties\nin seeing their rights fully recognized.90 The promotion of gender equality abroad in Mexican foreign policy\ncontrasts with alarming domestic statistics regarding women’s rights and security. On the one hand, a historic\n2021 court ruling decriminalized abortion.91 But, on the other, with lockdown policies put in place during\nthe Covid-19 pandemic, violence against women grew exponentially.92 This led the United Nations to declare\na “femicide pandemic” in Mexico, as murders of women reached a daily average of 11 in 2021. In the same\nyear, 3,700 women were killed, 887 of which were recognized as femicides. In 2020 the number was even\nhigher, with 1,000 femicides recorded.93 These astronomical numbers are paired with a lack of investigation\nand justice, as well as the stigmatization that victims of gender-based violence and their families often face,\ncontributing to an environment of insecurity, impunity, and discrimination.\nFigure 5: Female Homicides and Femicides\nYear Female Homicides Femicides % of Female Homicides Identified and Femicides\n2015 2,161 427 19.80%\n2016 2,835 648 22.90%\n2017 3,301 766 23.20%\n2018 3,678 919 25.00%\n2019 3,844 969 25.20%\n18 | Ryan C. The government has\ncut funding both for public shelters for gender-based violence victims on accusations of corruption, without\nevidence, and for national women’s institutes and national initiatives that have tried to protect women, especially\nin marginalized communities, such as Indigenous women.96 Furthermore, AMLO has sought to claim that Mexico’s\nfemicide challenge stems from the country’s past adherence to neoliberal policies. Despite his rhetoric, his actions\nsuggest not only that the protection of women and LGBTQ+ rights is very low on his agenda but also that, on the\ncontrary, his actions and policies have even intended to undermine a system that was already suffering.\nTHE HUMAN RIGHTS OUTLOOK FOR 2024\nThe weaknesses of Mexico’s justice system make the path forward difficult. Lack of resources, endemic\ncorruption, unwillingness to tackle impunity, and the complicity of state authorities in human rights abuses\nhighlight that the situation is complex and difficult to disentangle.\nThe impact is twofold: On the one hand, perpetrators not duly investigated, processed, or charged for their\ncrimes continue to pursue their activities with impunity. On the other hand, victims who do not receive justice\ncontinue to feel the psychological, economic, and social impact of what they have experienced.97 This creates\na cycle that only exacerbates human insecurity within the country. Unfortunately, without a change in political\nwill to focus on addressing and improving the justice and asylum systems and on protecting human rights, and\nin light of the alarming data regarding human rights violations in Mexico, the outlook through, and even after,\n2024 remains bleak. Human rights and protection of vulnerable populations do not seem to be a priority for\nthe 2024 candidates. Indeed, AMLO has shown that the proper rhetoric can stand in for genuine policies to\nimprove human rights protections. It is possible, but unlikely, that pressure from the international community,\nespecially close economic and interdependent partners, together with continuous agitation from civil society\norganizations could play a role in encouraging Mexican leaders to tamp down on abuses.\nReorganization of the Security Sector\nA consistent theme in AMLO’s leadership to date has been the mismatch between rhetoric and practice. AMLO’s\nelectoral campaign proclaimed the end of the Mexican army’s involvement in citizen security, with the aim of\nmaking Mexico a pacifist country. Yet AMLO has dutifully cultivated the armed forces’ loyalty while in office. He\nincreased the army budget by 22 percent and the budget for the marines by 17 percent, and he let the Mexican\n19 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024\n\narmy, navy, and the new national guard lead most of the country’s antidrug operations.98 He has also placed\npeople of confidence in leadership positions and diverted resources from other activities to the armed forces.\nAMLO’s goal when he came to office was to transform the security forces and establish a national guard, a\nbody with civilian command that would merge the military and the federal police and serve to target organized\ncrime and violence. In practice, the force has remained a de facto auxiliary military unit, with 76 percent of its\n115,000 members drawn from either SEDENA or SEMAR.99 In 2021 Mexico’s Senate formally acknowledged the\nnational guard’s military ties, incorporating the force under the Ministry of Defense in September 2022.\nAMLO has faced criticism for the inherently contradictory approach of strengthening militarization in Mexico\nwithout evidence it would result in more efficient management of public security and reduce violence.100 Such\ncritiques have not stopped him from deploying the national guard on the U.S.-Mexico border to deal with\nmigration, raising concerns from several human rights bodies over the excessive power concentrated in the\nhands of the armed forces.\nIn 2020 AMLO passed a decree that allowed the national guard to patrol the streets of Mexico until the end of\nhis mandate. A 2022 vote in the lower house extended the decree to 2028 through a constitutional amendment\nrevealing AMLO’s intent to authorize the use of the military into his successor’s mandate.101 Once more, the\nopposition condemned the move, but to no avail.102\nFigure 6: Origins of National Guard Personnel\nSEMAR\n16.7%\nFederal Police SEDENA\n22.8% 60.5%\nSource: “Censo Nacional de Segura Pública Federal” [National census of federal public security], Mexican National Institute of Statistics\nand Geography (INEGI), December 16, 2022, https://www.inegi.org.mx/app/saladeprensa/noticia.html?id=7878.\nMexico’s reliance on the army for internal security has also resulted in scandals and public outcry. For\ninstance, in March 2022, two additional army units were deployed in Nuevo Laredo, ostensibly to bring down\nrising levels of violence. Yet human rights activists quickly denounced the move, citing armed forces attacks\nagainst civilians who had nothing to do with organized crime.103 A similar episode occurred in 2021 when\nthe national guard mistakenly killed a mechanic of Nuevo Laredo and tried to cover up the error by offering\nmoney to his widow.104 SEDENA and the new national guard are listed among the 10 government bodies “most\nfrequently named in the disciplinary proceedings for presumed human rights violations.”105\nSuch incidents are not limited to the armed forces. In September 2019, police announced they had killed gunmen\nfrom the Northeast Cartel, but victims’ families revealed the police had actually murdered civilians not involved\nin drug trafficking.106 Moreover, in 2021 a police unit was charged with the massacre of 19 migrants in the desert.107\n20 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer\n\nIndeed, these episodes constitute further human rights violations that contribute to an environment characterized\nby insecurity and impunity. Corruption, complicity of public officials, and insufficient training and resources mean\nthat only 10 percent of crimes are reported and less than 16 percent of investigations are solved.108\nTHE SECURITY REFORM OUTLOOK FOR 2024\nConstitutional changes that allow the military to patrol the streets and handle public security until 2028\nhave already determined Mexico’s security outlook for 2024 and beyond. The growing militarization of the\ncountry under AMLO, accompanied by more violence and less security, is regrettably AMLO’s most enduring\npolicy.109 Subsequent governments will have to either accept this trend or actively seek to reverse it, fighting\nthe powerful security bureaucracies with their vested interests. It is also likely that even if the opposition were\nto take the presidency in 2024, a militarized approach would still be necessary to present a frontal challenge to\nthe entrenched criminal groups acting on the ground.\nPerhaps for this same reason, the security reform has influenced, and will likely continue to influence,\npolitics. The opposition blocked a constitutional reform to establish the national guard until the Institutional\nRevolutionary Party (PRI) decided to side with AMLO, breaking the stalemate and deceiving PAN. Although\nPAN and PRI have announced their commitment to run together in the 2024 elections, such episodes of\ndisagreement and disunity undermine the opposition’s chances of success and suggest that security reform\nand organization could prove a wedge issue for the opposition coalition.\nFigure 7: Defense Spending as a Percentage of Government Expenditures\nPercent of General Government Expenditure\n2.5%\n2.0\n1.5\n1.0\n1990 1995 2000 2005 2010 2015 2020\nSource: Stockholm International Peace Research Institute, “Military Expenditure (% of GDP) – Mexico,” World Bank Data, https://data.\nworldbank.org/indicator/MS.MIL.XPND.GD.ZS?locations=MX.\n21 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024", "excerpt_sha256": "5564433c13ad3840f86fc2a10dc942b51628bf3e71bad2283e93ce56cecbe8ba", "verification_status": "pending", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0051-E019", "source_id": "SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1", "url": "https://csis-website-prod.s3.amazonaws.com/s3fs-public/2023-07/230718_Berg_After_AMLO_0.pdf", "title": "After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024", "publisher": "Center for Strategic and International Studies", "raw_sha256": "112c3fb2368e464f70403bf4f07af82fb3b49ec04385a56a31a037931a4b98bd", "fact_summary": "CSIS reported budget cuts and political pressure affecting the INE, INAI, COFECE and CNDH; alleged attacks on NGOs, defenders, media and anti-corruption initiatives; and an electoral Plan B that reduced the electoral arbiter’s powers and was fast-tracked. Building an anti-\ncorruption, anti-neoliberal economic discourse while avoiding firm positions on controversial issues\nsuch as gay marriage or abortion rights, AMLO managed to appeal to voters across a wide range of political\norientations.110 Once in power, however, he used the excuse of combating corruption to justify his need to\nfully command the government and wield more power than his predecessors.111 Who will occupy the Palacio\nNacional in 2024 remains an open question, but regardless of the outcome, AMLO’s attacks on democratic\ninstitutions, the dynamics of the Morena party and the opposition, and U.S.-Mexico relations will heavily shape\nboth the campaign and post-campaign environments.\nAttacks against Democracy\nAMLO’s style has steadily become more populist, and his attacks against independent democratic institutions built\nover the past 30 years have led Mexico down a perilous route. Especially concerning has been his weaponization of\nanti-corruption rhetoric in Mexico, the crutch that supports many of his most controversial political projects.\nHis strategy to silence critics and avoid checks and balances has broken institutions, cutting their budgets and\nplacing loyalists in key positions of power. The INE, the Federal Economic Competition Commission (COFECE),\nand the INAI have all seen their budgets slashed.112 The proposal for the 2023 budget published in November 2022\nfit this trend. It cut the INE’s budget by nearly 4.5 billion pesos, the INAI’s by 18.6 million, and the COFECE’s by\n33.5 million.113 In the case of the National Human Rights Commission (CNDH), the controversial election of Rosario\nPiedra Ibarra, who openly supported AMLO’s political projects, as its head raised concerns over the extent to which\nthe commission could remain silent over human rights violations committed by the military and simultaneously\n22 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer\n\noperate independently from the government.114 Piedra spoke in favor of AMLO’s electoral reform, explicitly showing\nhow the commission is politically aligned with AMLO and how its autonomy has been compromised.115\nAlong the same lines, AMLO has denied human rights abuses and has directly attacked nongovernmental\norganizations, human rights defenders, women’s organizations, civil society movements, the media, and genuine\nanti-corruption initiatives.116 Some of his actions have undermined the government’s division of powers, verging\non illegality and unconstitutional action. These include his appointment of finance ministers before going through\nthe appropriate legislative procedure, weaponization of the judicial branch by threatening professors and scientists\nwith incarceration, organizing unlawful press conferences in support of his political party during the referendum\ncampaign, and using tax authority and never-ending audits to suppress corruption investigations.117\nOne of the most recent examples of blatant democratic backsliding is the electoral system reform AMLO\ncontinued to push for in an effort return to a time when the party in power controlled the electoral process.118\nAfter he abandoned his initial reform proposal due to lack of consensus in the Chamber of Deputies, AMLO\ndevised Plan B, which required only a simple majority for approval. This second proposal still reduced the\nsize of the organization, removed powers from the electoral arbiter limiting its ability to enforce electoral\nlaws, and softened sanctions for political parties. The plan was fast-tracked for approval in November 2022,\nundermining thorough analysis and discussion and circumventing the opposition almost entirely.119 The speed\nand coordination with which the reform came into being led many to conclude it had been AMLO’s plan all\nalong. On February 22, 2023, the plenary of the Senate approved the electoral reform without an eternal life\nclause, an article that allows the transfer of votes between parties forming a coalition in order to prevent\nthe disappearance of parties with little electoral strength, which struggled to find consensus.120 Indeed, the\nelectoral reform seems to have come as the most brazen of AMLO’s attacks against democracy, perhaps in\nan attempt to make the political context more favorable for the election of his preferred successor in 2024.\nThis has, in turn, provoked a forceful reaction among Mexican civil society, as there is unanimity among\nacademics, businesspeople, and political analysts that Mexican democracy rarely has sunk so low.121\nNot only did the electoral reform engender massive protests, but it also helped the opposition fully unify for\nthe first time since the beginning of AMLO’s mandate, bringing his approval rating to the lowest level since he\ncame to office.122 Popular pushback has been accompanied by resistance by some institutions remaining faithful\nto democratic principles. First among these is the Supreme Court. In April 2022 AMLO organized a referendum\non the president’s adequacy to remain in charge until the end of his mandate.123 Surprisingly, the court ruled\nin favor of the referendum regardless of the extremely low budget AMLO allocated to conducting it adequately.\nNevertheless, the Supreme Court demonstrated independence and eventually deemed the referendum invalid,\ndeclaring it undermined democracy by directly affecting citizens’ participation in the political process.124\nFurthermore, the opposition welcomed the appointment of Chief Justice Norma Lucía Piña Hernández in early\nJanuary 2023.125 As she did not belong to AMLO’s entourage, her election came as a positive sign for Mexican\ndemocracy. She is the first female chief justice and a supporter of the court’s independence and of human rights,\nespecially women’s rights.126 Together with other justices, many of whom have been criticized for siding with\nAMLO, she will review numerous pieces of legislation challenged by the opposition currently sitting before the\ncourt, including the decree extending the militarization of public security.127 Justice Alberto Pérez Dayán suspended\nthe first part of AMLO’s Plan B from entering into effect in the states where the electoral process had already\nbegun (Coahuila and State of Mexico). The court’s decision was subsequently validated as the INE organized two\nremarkably successful and transparent elections on June 4, demonstrating its continued trustworthiness and\nrelevance.128 The INE won further victories. In late June, the Supreme Court invalidated important parts of Plan B.\nIn response, AMLO promised to double down on Plan C, and promised to continue with his proposed constitutional\n23 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024\n\nreform that would allow the general public to elect judges and magistrates, in light of his belief that the judiciary\nrepresents opposition and conservative interests.\nMost recently, AMLO ordered senators to hold a session in a separate chamber to exclude the opposition. At\nthis unconstitutional session, the Senate passed 20 reforms in five hours on all manner of topics, including\nnew mining regulations that effectively shut down the industry in Mexico, ceding control and a majority of\nrevenues from the Maya Train and airports to the Mexican army. Senators also established a new tourism\nagency, 80 percent of whose fees would be allocated to the army. Many of the reforms, which included several\nconstitutional changes, run afoul of the USMCA. Such blatant violation of democratic procedure was evident in\npictures from the scene, which featured senators voting in the middle of the night on legislation they had not\nread, seated at plastic picnic tables without microphones.129\nTHE DEMOCRACY OUTLOOK FOR 2024\nWith the end of term of the INE’s 11 commissioners in 2023, AMLO will likely want to appoint more members\nloyal to him to further reduce the independence of Mexico’s electoral system.130 Some analysts fear that if the\nappointment is brought to a successful conclusion, together with the reform of the electoral process, Mexico’s\ndemocratic institutions will be further weakened, and the nation might not have free and fair elections in 2024.\nAMLO is dedicated to ensuring his preferred candidate is elected and continues his political agenda. In fact, he\nhas even entertained ideas of breaking the cardinal rule in Mexican politics and standing for a second term.131\nResistance by the opposition and the population plays a pivotal role in checking AMLO’s extension of power\nand his impact on democratic institutions. An even more crucial position is that of the Supreme Court, called\nto officially demonstrate its independence by voting on the constitutionality of Plan B, which is expected to\ntake place by June. This will continue to be key, especially as the 2024 electoral campaign gets closer and the\nMexican social, political, and economic context becomes more fragile.\nFigure 8: Mexico’s Democracy Index Score over Time\n8\nFlawed Democracy\n7\n6\n5\nHybrid Regime\n4\n2006 2008 2010 2012 2014 2016 2018 2020 2022\nSource: Economist Intelligence Unit (EIU), Democracy Index 2022 (EIU, 2022), https://www.eiu.com/n/campaigns/democracy-index-\n2022/#:~:text=Indicators,political%20participation%20and%20political%20culture.\nOn May 18, 2023, the Supreme Court denied AMLO’s claim that the permitting process for pet projects like the\nMaya Train could be expedited because they represented matters of “national security.” Mere hours after the\nverdict was handed down, a near-identical order was announced, redesignating the Maya Train, along with\n24 | Ryan C. At the same time, disputes over energy and genetically modified corn, limits on DEA cooperation, strained security relations and ideological disagreements created uncertainty for trade, investment and counternarcotics cooperation.", "question_ids": ["1B.4", "2B.10", "2B.5", "2B.7", "5B.10", "5B.4", "8B.10", "8B.9"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The report includes the 2023 North American Leaders’ Summit and ongoing 2023 disputes.", "ruler_attribution": "Foreign-policy and cooperation choices were attributed to López Obrador’s government, while bilateral outcomes reflected both governments.", "limitations": ["The source focuses on outlook and policy implications rather than a complete record of executed diplomatic decisions."], "start_unit": 29, "end_unit": 34, "locator": "page 29 through page 34", "exact_excerpt": "several other initiatives, as vital to national security.132 This incident suggests AMLO will not sit idly by in the\nevent his electoral reforms flounder in the courts, and he may attempt a similar maneuver to keep some or all\nof Plan B in place.\nElections and Party Dynamics\nAMLO’s populist, antiestablishment rhetoric helped lead the Morena party to victory in 2018. For the first\ntime, PRI, PAN, and the Party of the Democratic Revolution (PRD) combined obtained only 40 percent of the\nvote, compared to their previous 90 percent share.133 Morena has benefited from incredible popular support\nsince, though signs of decline began to emerge in the 2021 midterm elections.\nThe midterm elections decreased Morena’s supermajority to an absolute majority, which was interpreted by\nmany political analysts as positive for Mexico.134 In fact, the elections were marked by high attendance at polls\nand absence of fraud and violence. More than half of the population voted against Morena, suggesting the desire\nfor more options and willingness to support democracy.135 Although Morena won most gubernatorial races, the\nopposition made significant gains in the Chamber of Deputies.136 This came as a wake-up call for the opposition,\nrevealing its potential to represent an alternative to Morena if unified in a coalition. Even if Morena won more\nrepresentatives, it lost in bigger cities, which might indicate a decrease in support within the middle-class electorate\nit used to appeal to. Following AMLO’s repression of feminist protests, the women’s electorate also turned on him,\nanother sign of emerging disillusion with Morena that could have repercussions in future elections.\nOn April 10, 2022, AMLO held the first recall referendum in the history of Mexico. Both Morena and the president\ncampaigned in favor of the referendum and AMLO remaining in charge but reduced the budget of the INE for\ncarrying out such a referendum from 18 million pesos to 4 million. The lack of a clear legal framework; the\nopacity of the referendum’s text, whether in favor of or against AMLO; the budget scandal; and the illegal active\nparticipation of the political party involved in the organization of the referendum affected its legitimacy and\ncitizens’ participation. Less than 20 percent of the population voted, which undermined the referendum’s legality,\nas it required a threshold of 40 percent to be constitutionally valid.137 Further, 91.86 percent of those who voted\nexpressed support for AMLO, who considered it a major victory. His reaction to the results also led many to\nspeculate about his hidden intent to use the referendum as an instrument to prolong his mandate after 2024.\nIn 2023 AMLO’s approval rate remained around 60 percent, according to most opinion polls.138 Evidently,\nmany are still attracted to AMLO’s welfare program and direct contact with citizens through his mañaneras\n(daily press conferences), seduced by his narrative of helping the poor against corruption and the elites. Many\nrevere the president’s personal austerity and common touch. Others have instead realized that the state of\nthe Mexican economy and security is far worse now than before AMLO came into office and that his welfare\nprogram conceals cuts to healthcare and education and presents financial irregularities, with evidence of\ncorruption and wasted resources.139\nIn the context of fast-approaching presidential elections, AMLO’s goal is one of maintaining popularity and\nensuring his party remains in power after he finishes his mandate. His challenge lies in institutionalizing the\nparty and forging a stable path for succession, while convincing the polarized electorate that Morena still\nincarnates the alternative to the political establishment. As of writing, Morena had not yet chosen its official\ncandidate for 2024, but two figures had emerged as the most likely to replace AMLO.140\nClaudia Sheinbaum, current mayor of Mexico City, is AMLO’s favorite and the most likely to continue his\nagenda and style. However, her support, even within Morena’s electorate, is hampered by scandals, accidents,\n25 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024\n\nand criticism of her management of the public transportation system in the Mexican capital.141 She could\nappeal to AMLO’s base but would likely struggle to reunite those who are disillusioned with Morena and are\nstarting to look for other options.\nMarcelo Ebrard, current minister of foreign affairs, is seen as the preferred candidate among Morena\nsupporters.142 He is perceived as a competent figure both within and outside Morena and is more independent\nfrom AMLO than Sheinbaum. He could easily appeal to the middle class and convince a broader electorate.143 If\nhe is not chosen as Morena’s candidate, he may run as an independent candidate, which would make the 2024\nelectoral campaign even more dynamic.\nAMLO has also indicated a few other candidates are fit for pursuing his political project: Adán Augusto López\nHernández, secretary of the interior, and Senator Ricardo Monreal Ávila.144 Given there is no established\nmechanism for party primaries, AMLO seems to prefer a set of opinion polls that will make it easier to vote\nfor Sheinbaum, even though such a procedure may trigger questions about transparency and lead to one of\nthe candidates running as an independent.145 Neither candidate, however, possesses the same charisma and\npersonal touch as AMLO, meaning Morena’s narrative could still shift away from AMLO’s.\nFigure 9: Potential Candidate Approval Ratings among Party Members\nMorena’s Coalition\nClaudia Sheinbaum\nMarcelo Ebrard\nRicardo Monreal\nGerardo Fernández Noroña\nAdán Augusto López\nVa Por México\nMiguel Ángel Mancera\nLilly Téllez\nSantiago Creel\nBeatriz Paredes\nClaudia Ruiz Massieu\nSilvano Aureoles\nMovimiento Ciudadano\nLuis Donaldo Colosio Riojas\nEnrique Alfaro\nSamuel García\n0% 5% 10% 15% 20% 25% 30% 35%\nSource: Jorge Buendía, “Encuesta: Morena sigue adelante y gana con cualquier corcholata” [Poll: Morena remains ahead and wins\nwith any aspirant], El Universal, May 5, 2022, https://www.eluniversal.com.mx/nacion/encuesta-morena-sigue-adelante-y-gana-con-\ncualquier-corcholata/.\n26 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer\n\nMorena has also benefited from the opposition not yet forming a cohesive alternative for Mexican citizens.\nThe opposition currently holds just two out of the ten most populous states and is experiencing difficulties in\ndefining its leader.146 Yet, PAN-PRI-PRD have confirmed that they will run together as the alliance called Va Por\nMéxico, and PAN will be in charge of finding the best candidates to challenge Morena.147 AMLO’s recent blatant\nattacks on Mexico’s democratic institutions have helped the opposition reorient and reframe its strategy. Polls\nfrom January 2023 reveal that the opposition currently has an approval rating of 39 percent.148 Further threats\nto democracy could make the opposition stronger and increase its popular support. If Movimiento Ciudadano\n(MC) were to join the opposition coalition, the chances of beating Morena would be much higher. MC could\ntip the balance given its policies are similar to those of PAN and PRI, it appeals to young urban voters, and it\ncurrently controls two of Mexico’s most important states.149\nTHE ELECTORAL OUTLOOK FOR 2024\nWhile the question of who will be president in 2024 cannot be answered with any degree of certainty, the June\n2023 gubernatorial elections for the State of Mexico and Coahuila nevertheless carry important implications. In\nthe State of Mexico, a shaky coalition among the opposition parties contributed to a win by Morena candidate\nDelfina Gómez, who garnered a 52.7 percent majority despite having been found guilty of stealing pay from\nfederal employees to support her party during a previous bid for governor. While Morena’s victory in a state\nlong viewed as a PRI bastion has been cast as a sign of the inevitability of a Morena president in 2024, the party’s\ndefeat by the opposition Va por México coalition in Coahuila suggests such predictions are far from set in stone\nand underscores the value of coalition-building and effective candidate selection as key ingredients going into\n2024.150 In Coahuila, Morena was beset by difficulties and demonstrated lack of discipline, unceremoniously\ndumping candidates and making last-minute adjustments to lists after lagging in the polls. The lack of unity\namong the opposition and current opinion polls still indicate the likelihood of a Morena 2.0 mandate, at least as\nfar as the presidency is concerned. Nevertheless, challenges persist for AMLO in picking a successor, with the\npossibility of an internal split within Morena. When it comes to Congress, Morena seems likely to lose ground,\nushering in a possibly contentious environment dominated by obstructionism, public dissent, and inability to\nadvance policy. Should a successor from Morena ascend to the presidency, they will also need to grapple with\nAMLO's legacy, which already promises to exercise significant influence as all the party's current aspirants voice\ntheir support for creating a new \"Ministry of the Fourth Transformation\" (potentially headed by AMLO's son) to\ncarry on the outgoing president's vision for the country.\nU.S.-Mexico Relations\nAMLO’s friendly attitude toward Trump from 2018 to 2020 obscured an anti-American agenda. This became\neven clearer once President Joseph Biden was elected and AMLO refused to congratulate him.151 The two\ncountries are now maintaining a “strained partnership” characterized by unsuccessful diplomatic efforts and\ncontroversies over energy policy.152 In fact, contrary to the equal treatment for private companies and path\ntoward renewable energy established in the USMCA, AMLO proceeded to nationalize energy companies and\nlithium production. Canadian and U.S. concerns ended up in formal dispute settlement mechanisms, which\nbegan in July 2022.\nAMLO faces a dilemma regarding the USMCA. Increased economic cooperation with the United States and\nCanada has been interpreted as a sign of stability, boosting international investments in Mexico.153 Yet AMLO’s\nanti-American sentiment has shaped his agenda and has been exploited as a political element. To him, personal\npopularity and the question of his successor matter more than the relationship with the United States.154\n27 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024\n\nMigration issues also heavily shape the bilateral relationship. During his administration, Trump threatened\nto impose 5 percent tariffs on imports from Mexico if the country did not get more involved in controlling\nmigration from Central America. An agreement was reached providing for Mexico’s contribution to controlling\nmigration in exchange for noninterference from the United States in internal Mexican issues and AMLO’s way\nof governing.155 With authorities counting 2.76 million undocumented migrants in 2022, Biden has underscored\nhow Trump’s compromise with AMLO does not work anymore, though he has been reluctant to address\nMexican internal affairs.156\nU.S. president Joe Biden (L) is welcomed by his Mexican counterpart AMLO upon landing at Felipe Ángeles\nInternational Airport in Zumpango de Ocampo, north of Mexico City on January 8, 2023.\nPhoto Source: Claudio Cruz/AFP/Getty Images\nAnother controversial area in the U.S.-Mexico relationship touches on drug enforcement cooperation and\nsecurity. Both countries appear to be talking at cross-purposes. The United States is deeply concerned with\ndrug trafficking, especially fentanyl coming from Mexico, which is responsible for the vast majority of the over\n107,000 overdose deaths reported in the United States for 2021.157 Mexico, for its part, is primarily concerned\nwith the flow of guns from the United States and recently sued the United States for alleged failure to curb\nillegal arms trafficking to Mexico.158 Cooperation in this field has been undermined by several actions taken by\nAMLO’s government. In 2020 the National Security Law responded to U.S. prosecution of Mexican officials for\ncollaboration with illegal drug traffickers by limiting the scope of action of U.S. drug enforcement agents on\nMexican territory. In 2021 the Mexican government shut down an investigative unit that worked closely with\nthe DEA, complicating the agency’s task of passing sensitive information to Mexican agents.159\nWith the new Bicentennial Framework launched in January 2022 replacing the Mérida Initiative, which defined\nU.S.-Mexico security cooperation for over a decade, Mexico and the United States have tried to address organized\ncrime less from a military perspective and more from a public health and developmental perspective, together with\nactions to target labs and extradite leaders.160 However, InSight Crime criticizes the plan for remaining very similar\nto the Mérida Initiative.161 The plan’s kingpin strategy, with its excessive focus on targeting leaders, might increase\ninsecurity, as followers are as important as leaders in avoiding fractures within and fights between drug cartels.162\n28 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer\n\nSeveral other episodes have yielded an uneven track record for the diplomatic relationship between Mexico\nand the United States. In 2022, for instance, AMLO boycotted the Summit of the Americas hosted in Los\nAngeles. Officially, he cited opposition to the barring of Nicaragua, Cuba, and Venezuela from the summit,\nthough his refusal was likely linked to a desire to avoid critics of Mexico’s internal affairs.163 The White House\nmeeting between AMLO and Biden in July 2022 reassured the existence and importance of Mexico-U.S. ties.\nDespite their different styles, Mexico and the United States agreed on the need to work more closely together,\nreduce barriers to trade, and tackle food insecurity and immigration. Immediately after the meeting, Mexico\nagreed to spend over $1.5 billion on border infrastructure between 2022 and 2024.164\nFollowing the U.S.-Mexico High-Level Economic Dialogue in 2022, the two countries committed to enhance\ntheir supply chains and reduce risk of disruptions by nearshoring the manufacturing of critical products, such\nas semiconductors and personal protective equipment (PPE), to Mexico and addressing critical minerals.165\nThe North American Leaders’ Summit in January 2023 was also deemed positive by observers for advancing\ncooperation, though it still took place under a cloud of tense trade negotiations.166\nDespite this gradual diplomatic rapprochement, the United States and Mexico appear ideologically divergent.\nAMLO has expressed a wish to see the Organization of American States reformed, accusing it of being\nexcessively dominated by the United States. The Mexico State Morena Youth group has supported the\nRussian invasion of Ukraine, while AMLO has criticized NATO’s actions. At the same time, Mexico has signed\nan agreement with Russia on space cooperation and has diplomatically supported dictatorships such as in\nVenezuela. This has further complicated every other aspect of the U.S.-Mexico partnership.167\nTHE BILATERAL OUTLOOK FOR 2024\nAlthough Biden has not been vocal that the U.S.-Mexico partnership is in danger and has opted not to criticize\nAMLO’s domestic practices, AMLO has brought Mexico ideologically further from the United States than it\nhas been in a long time. On the one hand, security cooperation and diplomacy have faced several ups and\ndowns that can be expected to continue in the final year of the AMLO and Biden administrations, given\ntheir inherently different styles and political agendas. On the other hand, trade and economic integration\nhave reached very high levels, with the two sides talking about aligning incentives in key industries such as\nsemiconductors. In 2022 Mexico was the United States’ second-largest trading partner in the trade of goods\nand services, while the United States accounted for 80 percent of Mexican goods exports.168 Recent increases\nin bilateral trade portend a trend that is likely to remain. In addition, with growing opportunities to relocate\nindustries from Asia to Latin America to avoid supply chain disruptions, Mexico has become key in attracting\ninvestors, underlining the expected expansion of U.S.-Mexico economic relations in the near future.169\nIn 2024 the core issues for the U.S.-Mexico relationship are anticipated to remain the same: Mexico and the\nUnited States will have to cooperate on trade, investment, full implementation of the USMCA, drug trafficking,\nsecurity, migration, and especially border control. However, the way in which such cooperation will manifest\ncould vary significantly. In 2024 both the United States and Mexico will elect new presidents. AMLO’s departure\ncould already be significant for the partnership, and no matter his success in designating a successor, whoever\nenters the Palacio Nacional next year will undoubtedly seek to leave their mark on Mexico’s foreign policy.\nMexican domestic and foreign policy—in particular the extent to which Mexico manages to control its borders—\ncould, in turn, affect U.S. elections, as border policy remains one of the Republican Party’s main concerns.\nMeanwhile, proponents within the U.S. Congress who have pushed legislative initiatives authorizing military\nforce, including air strikes, against criminal organizations operating in Mexico would do well to remember one\nanalyst’s observation: “Mexico is a target-rich environment, not for U.S. missiles, but for sanctions.”170\n29 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024\n\nIn addition, political relations will determine the extent to which Mexico can secure nearshoring\nopportunities, as well as investments in minerals, semiconductors, and other key industries that now seem to\nbroaden the potential of U.S.-Mexico economic relations. At the same time, Mexico may continue to pursue\nits anti-American sentiment aiming to avoid undue interference in internal affairs and seeking the support\nof countries with similar agendas.171 The more the country becomes militarized and the less secure and\ndemocratic it becomes, the more its relations with the United States could be eroded.172 In any case, Mexico\nwill still need pragmatism, as it cannot neglect its dependence on access to the U.S. market and the great\neconomic and trade opportunities established under the USMCA. Ultimately, geographic proximity, economic\ninterdependence, and positive day-to-day cooperation will play a role in keeping the two countries close,\noutlining a bright spot in an otherwise gloomy picture.\n30 | Ryan C. The presentation also identified high inflation, incomplete recovery, weak investment and uncertainty from global tightening and the Ukraine conflict.", "question_ids": ["5B.10", "5B.3", "5B.9", "8B.7"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The presentation concerns February 2022 conditions and is contextual rather than direct 2023 evidence.", "ruler_attribution": "It evaluates Mexico’s macroeconomic position during López Obrador’s administration but expressly states that the views are the author’s and not necessarily Banco de México’s institutional position.", "limitations": ["The source predates 2023 and does not attribute monetary policy decisions directly to the president."], "start_unit": 4, "end_unit": 8, "locator": "page 4 through page 8", "exact_excerpt": "A challenging global economic outlook\n\n\n• At the beginning of 2022, the world faces a challenging economic outlook, characterized by:\n\n   1. An uneven and uncomplete economic recovery across countries, regions and economic sectors.\n\n   2. A high global inflation phenomenon.\n\n   3. The prospect of the start of a tightening cycle of global monetary and financial conditions.\n\n   4. The economic and financial uncertainty derived from the current situation in Ukraine.\n\n\n• The outlook is particularly challenging for those emerging economies that currently experience a\n  weak recovery and/or do not have strong macroeconomic fundamentals.\n\n\n\n\n                                                The Mexican Economy in 2022: Outlook and Challenges   4\n\nUneven recovery\n• The world economic recovery is still uncomplete.\n• There is heterogeneity in the speed of recovery across countries and regions.\n     Some countries are currently experiencing high growth rates, while others have weak macroeconomic fundamentals.\n\n• Mexico is among those countries where the recovery has been somewhat slow, yet it has solid macroeconomic fundamentals.\n "url": "https://www.coneval.org.mx/InformesPublicaciones/Documents/Informe_Programas_prioritarios_2022.pdf", "title": "Informe de monitoreo de programas prioritarios 2022", "publisher": "CONEVAL", "raw_sha256": "522fcd23dda552bacc621a5eb549443d13ce51cc224e2314e385f0c5a504977a", "fact_summary": "CONEVAL reported that the administration’s 16 priority social programs had a 2022 original budget of 506.543 billion pesos, 71% above 2019 in real terms. In 2021 they reached 27 million people, or 64% of their identified target population, while 56% of programs achieved adequate or outstanding effective target compliance.", "question_ids": ["6B.1", "6B.10", "6B.2", "6B.3", "6B.5", "8B.10", "8B.3", "8B.6", "8B.7"], "polarity": "favorable", "period_fit": "The report was published in October 2022 and covers results through 2021, providing pre-2023 evidence.", "ruler_attribution": "The programs were established and funded under the López Obrador administration.", "limitations": ["The report does not measure 2022 or 2023 outcomes and coverage calculations depend on available administrative data."], "start_unit": 9, "end_unit": 10, "locator": "page 9 through page 10", "exact_excerpt": "Resumen ejecutivo\n\n          •      Con el objetivo de atender las principales problemáticas sociales, en 2019 se estableció\n                 un conjunto de 17 programas prioritarios de desarrollo social. A lo largo de la actual admi-\n                 nistración, algunos programas han sido eliminados de la lista y otros más se han incluido,\n                 estos cambios dan como resultado la existencia de 16 programas prioritarios de desarrollo\n                 social para el año 2022. La operación de estos se encuentra a cargo de seis dependencias\n                 federales, la Secretaría de Bienestar es la que mayor número de programas tiene a su cargo\n                 (seis programas).\n\n\n          •      En 2022, el presupuesto original de los programas prioritarios es de $506,543 millones de\n                 pesos (que representa el 40 % del presupuesto total asignado a todos los programas y accio-\n                 nes sociales), lo cual equivale a un incremento, en términos reales, del 71 %, respecto al pre-\n                 supuesto asignado en 2019. El Programa Pensión para el Bienestar de las Personas Adultas\n                 Mayores (S176) obtuvo el mayor presupuesto original con $238,014.73 millones de pesos y el\n                 Programa para el Bienestar de las Personas en Emergencia Social o Natural (U011) obtuvo\n                 el menor presupuesto original con $510.59 millones de pesos, ambos son coordinados por\n                 la Secretaría de Bienestar.\n\n\n          •      El Diagnóstico de Matrices 20221 reveló que, en general, se observa una mejoría en cuanto\n                 al diseño de la MIR de los programas prioritarios. En 2022, el 50 % obtuvo una calificación de\n                 diseño adecuada o destacada, esto representa un aumento de 24 puntos porcentuales en\n                 comparación con 2020. Como consecuencia, se observa también una disminución signifi-\n                 cativa de los programas que tienen matrices con oportunidad de mejora; en 2020 había\n                 ocho programas en este rango y para 2022 solo dos mantienen este nivel.\n\n\n          •      De los 16 programas prioritarios de desarrollo social, el 75% lograron identificar de manera\n                 total o parcial la temática que atienden. En el periodo 2019-2022, ocho registraron cambios\n                 en sus temáticas de atención: tres de los cuales fueron producto de un cambio de enfoque\n                 de los programas; tres más pasaron de no identificar una temática a poder identificarla de\n                 manera clara y los dos restantes perdieron claridad en la identificación de este aspecto.\n\n\n\n1\n    El Diagnóstico de Matrices 2022 puede consultarse en la siguiente liga: https://www.coneval.org.mx/coordinacion/IMPPS/Paginas/Diagnosticos-de-Monitoreo-de-progra-\n    mas-y-politicas-sociales.aspx\n\n\n\n\n                                       8              Informe de monitoreo de programas prioritarios 2022: estado actual al cuarto año de la Administración y resultados alcanzados en 2021\n\n•      A través del análisis de poblaciones objetivo, los programas prioritarios se clasificaron en\n                  seis grupos: jóvenes, niñas y niños, productores, población en condiciones de vulnerabilidad,\n                  personas adultas mayores y personas con discapacidad. No obstante, aún hay cuatro que\n                  no tienen claridad respecto a la población objetivo que buscan atender, a este grupo se les\n                  asignó la categoría de “población objetivo no identificada”.\n\n\n           •      Al analizar la concentración presupuestal por grupo poblacional, se observa que las perso-\n                  nas adultas mayores tienen la mayor parte del presupuesto de los programas prioritarios con\n                  el 47 %. En contraste, la población en condiciones de vulnerabilidad2 es el grupo con menor\n                  presupuesto ya que únicamente tiene el 1 %.\n\n\n           •      La valoración del Enfoque de Resultados (EdR) de los 16 programas prioritarios mostró que\n                  el 63 % (10) de estos lograron identificar, de manera conjunta, tanto su problemática pública\n                  como su población objetivo. De este conjunto, seis que en 2019 no identificaban estos ele-\n                  mentos o lo hacían de manera parcial, lograron identificarlos plenamente en 2022; no obs-\n                  tante, tres programas que identificaban su problema público en 2019 perdieron precisión en\n                  su identificación en 2022.\n\n\n           •      Los principales retos del EdR se encuentran en la identificación de la población objetivo y la\n                  medición de resultados. En la primera, hubo un retroceso respecto a 2019, ya que 14 de los\n                  16 programas prioritarios identificaban de manera clara a su población, mientras que, en\n                  2022, solo 12 programas la identifican. En cuanto a la medición de resultados se encontró\n                  que el 31 % de los programas aún no cuentan con indicadores apropiados para monitorear\n                  el logro de sus objetivos centrales.\n\n\n           •      El estudio de cobertura reveló que de forma general, los programas prioritarios, atendieron\n                  en 2021 a 27 millones de personas mexicanas, cifra equivalente al 64 % de la población\n                  objetivo de los programas. De 2020 a 2021, los programas que incrementaron en mayor me-\n                  dida su población atendida son Pensión para el Bienestar de las Personas Adultas Mayores\n                  (S176), Programa de Becas de Educación Básica para el Bienestar Benito Juárez (S072) y\n                  Beca Universal para Estudiantes de Educación Media Superior Benito Juárez (S311). Entre los\n                  tres programas incorporaron a 5.6 millones de personas beneficiarias, es decir, un incremen-\n                  to del 26 % respecto del año previo. De manera coincidente, estos programas son los que\n                  tuvieron el porcentaje de cobertura más alto, por encima del 70 % en 2021.\n\n\n           •      Los resultados del Cumplimiento Efectivo de Metas demuestran que el 56 % de los progra-\n                  mas prioritarios tuvieron un cumplimiento adecuado o destacado en 2021. Al comparar los\n                  resultados 2020 y 2021, se observa que cuatro programas que contaban con un nivel bajo\n                  o deficiente en 2020 lograron ubicarse como adecuados o destacados en 2021, en este\n                  grupo se encuentran los dos programas prioritarios coordinados por la Sedatu.\n2\n    La población objetivo en condiciones de vulnerabilidad para efectos del presente análisis, es aquella que por su entorno socioeconómico presenta alguna condición que\n    vulnere su acceso efectivo a algún derecho social (artículo 6to de la Ley General de Desarrollo Social (LGDS), y que no se encuentra dentro de las siguientes poblaciones:\n    adultos mayores, jóvenes, personas con discapacidad y/o productores\n\n\n\n\n                                                              Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social   9", "excerpt_sha256": "8bd42e41dd6ca77fb21dc0343ec4db799903fe16eb89bd70ac562a8dd8ee8c86", "verification_status": "pending", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0051-E024", "source_id": "SRC-c27c10e952de9faf", "url": "https://www.coneval.org.mx/InformesPublicaciones/Documents/Informe_Programas_prioritarios_2022.pdf", "title": "Informe de monitoreo de programas prioritarios 2022", "publisher": "CONEVAL", "raw_sha256": "522fcd23dda552bacc621a5eb549443d13ce51cc224e2314e385f0c5a504977a", "fact_summary": "CONEVAL found major improvement in the design of priority-program results matrices: programs with adequate or outstanding overall design rose from 26% in 2020 to 50% in 2022. However, six programs lacked a consolidated results orientation, 31% lacked suitable outcome indicators, and health and university programs remained especially weak. The health program’s objective shifted from delivering effective and equitable health access to transferring resources to states, reducing clarity about intended outcomes.", "question_ids": ["6B.2", "6B.5", "8B.1", "8B.2", "8B.6", "8B.7", "8B.8"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The assessment covers 2022 program design and is relevant to the 2023 administrative baseline.", "ruler_attribution": "The priority programs were part of the federal administration’s social-policy architecture.", "limitations": ["The report evaluates program design and monitoring, not the president’s personal decisions or 2023 service outcomes."], "start_unit": 20, "end_unit": 26, "locator": "page 20 through page 26", "exact_excerpt": "Calidad de la Matriz de Indicadores para Resultados de los\nProgramas Prioritarios3\n\nDe manera bienal, el CONEVAL realiza el Diagnóstico de Matrices con base en la metodología de\nanálisis de la calidad de los objetivos e indicadores de los programas presupuestarios (ver figura 2)\nen los cuatro niveles que conforman la MIR: Fin, Propósito, Componente y Actividades. En 2020, solo el 26 % tuvo un diseño adecuado o destacado, mientras que en\n2022 la proporción subió al 50%. De igual manera, aumentó la categoría de moderados, que pasó\ndel 25 % en 2020 al 38 % en 2022, un incremento de 13 puntos porcentuales. También se observa una\ndisminución significativa en los programas con una MIR con oportunidad de mejora, en 2020 ocho\nprogramas formaban parte de este grupo, para 2022 solamente dos se mantienen en este nivel, estos\nprogramas son Universidades para el Bienestar Benito Juárez García (U083) de la SEP y Atención a la\nSalud y Medicamentos Gratuitos para la Población sin Seguridad Social Laboral (U013).\n\n\n\n\n3\n    Consúltese la metodología del diagnóstico de matrices en: https://www.coneval.org.mx/coordinacion/Documents/monitoreo/informes/Diagnostico_MIR_2020.pdf\n\n\n\n\n                                                                Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social        19\n\nDe los programas que en 2020 tenían oportunidad de mejora, Fertilizantes (S292), coordinado por la\nSader, pasó de tener una MIR dentro de esta categoría a una MIR con diseño adecuado. Este cambio\nse explica debido a que el programa logró formular indicadores con un nivel destacado en 2022, es\ndecir, sus indicadores cumplen con los criterios de calidad definidos por el CONEVAL para dar un se-\nguimiento adecuado a sus objetivos. No obstante, aún tiene pendiente analizar si con la sola entrega\nde fertilizantes los productores que forman parte de su población podrían incrementar su producción.\n\n\nPor su parte, Producción para el Bienestar (S293), también coordinado por esa dependencia, pasó\nde tener una MIR con oportunidad de mejora en 2020, a una MIR con diseño destacado en 2022. Lo\nanterior debido a que logró un diseño destacado, tanto en objetivos como en el planteamiento de in-\ndicadores, por tanto, consiguió consolidar su matriz de indicadores con la calidad y solidez suficiente\npara monitorear su desempeño.\n\n\nGráfica 3. Los programas Jóvenes Construyendo el Futuro (S280) de la STPS y Beca\nUniversal para Estudiantes de Educación Media Superior Benito Juárez (S311) de la SEP, pasaron de\ntener indicadores con un diseño con oportunidad de mejora, a un diseño destacado. Por su parte,\nlos programas Fertilizantes (S292), Producción para el Bienestar (S293), ambos de la Sader, y Jóvenes\nEscribiendo el Futuro (S283) de la SEP, que en 2020 tuvieron indicadores con calidad moderada, en\n2022 lograron una calidad destacada.\n\n\nNo obstante, aún hay programas que necesitan mejorar esta área. En este escenario se encuentra el\nprograma Universidades para el Bienestar Benito Juárez García (U083), que pasó de tener indicadores\ncon calidad moderada en 2020 a indicadores con oportunidad de mejora en 2022. Asimismo, el Pro-\ngrama de Mejoramiento Urbano (S273), se mantiene en la categoría moderada tanto en 2020 y 2022.\n\n\n\n                              20       Informe de monitoreo de programas prioritarios 2022: estado actual al cuarto año de la Administración y resultados alcanzados en 2021\n\nPor su parte, Atención a la Salud y Medicamentos Gratuitos para la Población sin Seguridad Social\nLaboral (U013) de la Secretaría de Salud es el único programa prioritario que tiene indicadores con\noportunidad de mejora desde 2020 y no ha logrado superar esta condición.\n\n\nEn la figura 3 se muestra cuál ha sido la transición de la calidad de los indicadores de los programas\nentre el 2020 y 2022. Se observa que el número de programas que cuentan con calidad destacada\naumentó de cuatro en 2020 a ocho en 2022. Dentro de las mejoras también se identifica que en 2020\nninguno de los programas contaba con calidad adecuada en sus indicadores, mientras que en 2022\nexisten tres programas en esta categoría, dos de ellos pasaron de tener semáforo amarillo en 2020 a\nverde en 2022. De igual manera, dos programas que contaban con semáforo rojo en 2020 pasaron\na amarillo en 2022. Por último, en 2022 solo dos programas tienen indicadores con oportunidad de\nmejora, mientras que en 2020 había cinco en esta condición.\n\n\nFigura 3. Para identificar las temáticas de atención de una intervención pública, el CONEVAL realizó un\nanálisis de objetivos centrales (Propósito), a través del cual se determinó si en el Propósito de cada\nprograma se formuló de manera parcial o total una problemática pública a resolver. En este análisis\nse identificaron las siguientes temáticas: bienestar económico, bienestar social, carencias sociales,\nciencia y tecnología, desarrollo cultural, medio ambiente, mejora educativa, mejora en salud, mejora\nlaboral, productividad y gestión. Cabe señalar que si el objetivo carece de claridad en la identifica-\nción del problema público que se pretende resolver se clasifica dentro de la categoría gestión.\n\n\nDe los 16 programas prioritarios vigentes, 12 (75 %) de ellos lograron identificar de manera total o par-\ncial la temática de su problema central y cuatro (25 %) fueron clasificados con un objetivo de gestión,\nes decir, no se logró identificar el resultado esperado en la población.\n\n\nEn la figura 4, se muestra la distribución de programas por temática de atención, donde se observa\nque las más atendidas por los programas prioritarios, sin considerar aquellos cuyo objetivo aún se\nclasifica como de gestión, son el bienestar social y la mejora educativa con tres programas cada una.\n\n\nEn la temática de gestión —donde se incluyen a todos los programas que no tienen claridad en la\nidentificación del problema público que pretenden resolver— se encuentran programas como Univer-\nsidades para el Bienestar Benito Juárez García (U083) Jóvenes Construyendo el Futuro (S280) y Aten-\nción a la Salud y Medicamentos Gratuitos para la Población sin Seguridad Social Laboral (U013). Una\nvez que estos programas definan su objetivo central con enfoque de resultados, se podrían ubicar en\nlas temáticas de mejora educativa, productividad o mejora en salud, respectivamente.\n\n\nUn ejemplo de un programa que logró estructurar su objetivo central con enfoque de resultados fue\nSembrando Vida (S287), que pasó de un objetivo de gestión a uno de atención a carencias sociales.\nEn su Propósito actual, el programa busca que su población objetivo cubra sus necesidades alimen-\nticias básicas a través de la entrega de: apoyos en especie para la producción agroforestal; acom-\npañamiento social y técnico para la implementación del programa y aportaciones económica a sus\nbeneficiarios.\n\n\n\n                                     Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social   23\n\nFigura 4. Tres\nde ellos lo hicieron debido a un cambio de enfoque que experimentaron durante este periodo; por\nejemplo, el Programa Pensión de las Personas con Discapacidad Permanente (S286), pasó de buscar\nmejorar la calidad de vida de su población objetivo, a “buscar mejorar el ingreso monetario” de esta\npoblación.\n\n\nPor su parte, tres programas han ganado claridad en la identificación de su problemática pública\na resolver, al pasar de un objetivo de gestión a una temática claramente identificada. Ejemplo de\nello es Producción para el Bienestar (S293) que pasó de tener dos objetivos centrales4 a definir un\núnico objetivo que busca como resultado que los pequeños y medianos productores incrementen el\nrendimiento de sus cultivos o unidades de producción. Los otros dos programas que mejoraron son\nFertilizantes (S292), que pasó de tener una temática de gestión a una de productividad; y Sembrando\nVida (S287) que pasó de un objetivo de gestión a uno enfocado a resolver la carencia alimentaria\nde su población objetivo.\n\n\n\n\n4\n    En la Metodología de Marco Lógico se establece que el problema que busca resolver una intervención pública debe ser único, ya que el definir más de uno ocasiona que\n    los esfuerzos y recursos del programa se dispersen, además de dificultar la tarea de monitoreo y evaluación. Para más información consultar: https://www.coneval.org.mx/\n    Informes/Coordinacion/Publicaciones%20oficiales/GUIA_PARA_LA_ELABORACION_DE_MATRIZ_DE_INDICADORES.pdf\n\n\n\n\n                                          24              Informe de monitoreo de programas prioritarios 2022: estado actual al cuarto año de la Administración y resultados alcanzados en 2021\n\nDos programas sociales perdieron claridad en el resultado que buscan alcanzar: Universidades para\nel Bienestar Benito Juárez García (U083) y Atención a la Salud y Medicamentos Gratuitos para la Po-\nblación sin Seguridad Social Laboral (U013).\n\n\nEl primero perdió claridad en la delimitación del resultado que se busca lograr. Al inicio del programa,\nel propósito fue que “las personas con bachillerato terminado accedieran a los servicios de las univer-\nsidades Benito Juárez”; en 2022 el propósito del programa busca: “Superar la exclusión educativa de\ntipo superior contribuye a disminuir la desigualdad en comunidades y municipios”. Con este objetivo\nse pierde claridad de la población sobre la cual busca generar un resultado; es decir, requiere especi-\nficar las características socioeconómicas y demográficas particulares de las personas que presentan\nel problema que el programa quiere solucionar. En ese caso, hay una oportunidad de mejora en la\nclaridad con que el programa operacionalice el: “superar la exclusión educativa”. Tal como está plan-\nteada, podría registrar sus resultados en diversas formas, por lo que se considera ambigua.\n\n\nEn cuanto al Programa Atención a la Salud y Medicamentos Gratuitos para la Población sin Seguridad\nSocial (U013), al inicio de su operación, su propósito se orientaba a que “la población sin seguridad social\ntuviera acceso efectivo, pleno y equitativo a la atención a la salud y a los medicamentos gratuitos”,\nque formaba parte de la temática de mejora en salud; desde 2021, tiene como objetivo central, la\ntransferencia de recursos a las entidades federativas”. La oportunidad de mejora en el Propósito de\neste programa radica también en la claridad de la población objetivo que presenta el problema, así\ncomo en el cambio o resultado que se busca generar en ella.\n\n\nFigura 5. {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0051-E025", "source_id": "SRC-c27c10e952de9faf", "url": "https://www.coneval.org.mx/InformesPublicaciones/Documents/Informe_Programas_prioritarios_2022.pdf", "title": "Informe de monitoreo de programas prioritarios 2022", "publisher": "CONEVAL", "raw_sha256": "522fcd23dda552bacc621a5eb549443d13ce51cc224e2314e385f0c5a504977a", "fact_summary": "Program coverage in 2021 varied sharply: pensions and major education scholarships exceeded 70% coverage, while Jóvenes Construyendo el Futuro, Universidades para el Bienestar and emergency assistance were below 17%. Some apparent improvements resulted from redefining the target population; for example, disability-pension coverage rose from 14% to 65% largely because the target population fell by 74%.", "question_ids": ["6B.10", "6B.2", "6B.3", "6B.5", "8B.7", "8B.8"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The findings concern 2021 results, reported in 2022, and provide context for 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The programs were administered under López Obrador’s government.", "limitations": ["Coverage changes may reflect measurement and target-population revisions rather than actual equivalent welfare gains."], "start_unit": 38, "end_unit": 40, "locator": "page 38 through page 40", "exact_excerpt": "La cobertura es un indicador que se estima mediante un porcentaje; por el lado del numerador se en-\ncuentra la población atendida por un programa presupuestario en un periodo determinado, mientras\nque por el lado del denominador se contabiliza a la población objetivo del programa en ese mismo\nperiodo. Es un indicador sumamente relevante puesto que permite observar en una sola cifra, la mag-\nnitud de la problemática que enfrenta un programa social.10\n\n\nPara realizar este cálculo, se utilizaron las cifras reportadas por los programas prioritarios en la Cuenta\nPública 2021; mientras que para aquellos programas que no contaban con información referente a su\npoblación atendida o población objetivo, se utilizó la información disponible dentro del Inventario\nCONEVAL.11 Como resultado de este análisis, se observa que los programas prioritarios atendieron a\n27 millones de mexicanos en 2021, es decir, en general, el 21 % de la población nacional. Si la pobla-\nción atendida se compara con respecto a la población objetivo (población que enfrenta la proble-\nmática pública que busca resolver el programa), se obtiene una cobertura del 64 %.\n\n\nDe 2020 a 2021, la población atendida por los programas prioritarios se ha incrementado en 26 %, lo\nanterior, principalmente como resultado del aumento en el número de beneficiarios de los programas\nPensión para el Bienestar de las Personas Adultas Mayores (S176), Programa de Becas de Educación\nBásica para el Bienestar Benito Juárez (S072) y Beca Universal para Estudiantes de Educación Media\nSuperior Benito Juárez (S311). Los tres programas incorporaron a 5.6 millones de beneficiarios en este\nperiodo. Así mismo, junto con Producción para el Bienestar (S293), fueron los programas prioritarios\ncon el mayor número de beneficiarios en 2021.\n\n\n\n10\n   El presente apartado se enfoca a analizar el indicador solicitado en el numeral 12 de los Lineamientos para el proceso de seguimiento y modificación extemporánea de\n   los Instrumentos de Seguimiento del Desempeño de los Programas presupuestarios para el Ejercicio Fiscal 2022. El método de cálculo genérico para dicho indicador es el\n   siguiente: Cobertura del Pp=((Población atendida por el Pp)/(Población objetivo del Pp))×100\n11\n   Inventario Coneval de Programas y Acciones Desarrollo Social: https://www.coneval.org.mx/evaluacion/ipfe/Paginas/default.aspx\n\n\n\n\n                                                           Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social   37\n\nFigura 9. las va-\nriaciones del indicador se pueden dar por ajustes en las características empleadas para determinar\na las poblaciones objetivo (que incrementan o disminuyen este universo), por cambios en el número\nde población atendida por los programas, o por cambios simultáneos en ambas mediciones.\n\n\n\n                                                38             Informe de monitoreo de programas prioritarios 2022: estado actual al cuarto año de la Administración y resultados alcanzados en 2021\n\nPor otro lado, es preciso mencionar que en solo 11 de los 16 programas prioritarios fue posible comparar\n su cobertura entre 2019 y 2021. En siete de los once programas con coberturas comparables en este\n periodo, se incrementó este indicador. Ejemplo de ello es el programa Pensión para el Bienestar de Perso-\n nas con Discapacidad Permanente (S286) que registró un aumento de 51 puntos porcentuales, al pasar\n de una cobertura del 15 % en 2019 a una del 65 % en 2021. No obstante, este incremento es resultado\n del ajuste de su población objetivo, que pasó de contabilizar a 5.7 millones de personas en 2019 a 1.5\n millones en 2021. Otro programa que reporta incrementos en su cobertura es Precios de Garantía a Pro-\n ductos Alimentarios Básicos (S290), al pasar de una cobertura del 33 % en 2019 a una del 51 % en 2021.\n\n\n Por su parte, tres programas disminuyeron su porcentaje de atención, entre ellos se encuentra el pro-\n grama Universidades para el Bienestar Benito Juárez García (U083), que pasó de una cobertura del\n 14 % en 2019 a 10 % en 2021; en términos absolutos, esto representó una disminución de casi 6,000\n beneficiarios. Otro programa con disminución en su cobertura fue Producción para el Bienestar (S293)\n que, a pesar de registrar niveles altos de cobertura, pasó de un 91 % en 2019 a un 88 % en 2021, esta\n situación se debió al incremento en el universo de su población objetivo en este periodo.12\n\n\n El único programa que mantuvo constante esta medición fue Pensión para el Bienestar de las Per-\n sonas Adultas Mayores (S176), coordinado por la Secretaría de Bienestar, al contar con un nivel de\n cobertura del 94 % en 2019 y 2021, lo anterior debido a que su población atendida y su población\n objetivo crecieron en la misma proporción.\n\n\n Gráfica 9. "https://www.coneval.org.mx/InformesPublicaciones/Documents/Informe_Programas_prioritarios_2022.pdf", "title": "Informe de monitoreo de programas prioritarios 2022", "publisher": "CONEVAL", "raw_sha256": "522fcd23dda552bacc621a5eb549443d13ce51cc224e2314e385f0c5a504977a", "fact_summary": "CONEVAL found that 56% of priority programs had adequate or outstanding target compliance in 2021, but the health program with the second-largest budget had deficient compliance, while the largest-budget adult-pension program was outstanding. Several programs deteriorated from 2020, and budget allocation showed almost no relationship to effective target compliance. The report identified weak target-setting, unclear populations and inconsistent sustained performance as continuing challenges.", "question_ids": ["5B.6", "5B.7", "6B.10", "6B.2", "6B.5", "8B.10", "8B.6", "8B.7", "8B.8"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The report evaluates 2021 results and 2022 budgets, serving as evidence about implementation capacity entering 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The programs and budget choices belonged to the federal administration under López Obrador.", "limitations": ["The report does not establish why budget allocations were made or whether later corrective action occurred."], "start_unit": 42, "end_unit": 51, "locator": "page 42 through page 51", "exact_excerpt": "Metodología\n\nCuando se crea un programa o acción social, este responde a un objetivo central o Propósito y a\nciertos objetivos específicos que el gobierno mexicano busca lograr en un determinado periodo.\nPara conocer si el cumplimiento de estos objetivos se está llevando a cabo, los programas formulan\nindicadores y establecen metas, cuya consecución se monitorea a través de estas mediciones. Para\ncontar con una valoración de este proceso, el CONEVAL desarrolló la metodología del Cumplimiento\nEfectivo de Metas (CEM), la cual analiza en qué medida los programas sociales cumplen de manera\nefectiva lo que pretenden lograr. La metodología considera exclusivamente los indicadores de nivel\nPropósito y Componentes que están incluidos en la Matriz de Indicadores para Resultados (MIR) de\nlos programas y acciones sociales.\n\n\nEl CEM, por tanto, valora el cumplimiento de metas de cada indicador, la calidad de estas medicio-\nnes y la congruencia en los ajustes de metas que pudo experimentar el programa por ajustes en su\npresupuesto. El cálculo del CEM se realiza en dos fases: 1) la valoración del nivel de logro de cada\nuno de los indicadores de su MIR asociados al objetivo principal y a los entregables que otorgan a\nsu población beneficiaria y 2) la estimación del CEM expresada en una escala 0-1.13 Lo anterior se\ndescribe en la siguiente figura:\n\n\nFigura 10. Metodología del CEM\n\n\n                                                                         Cumplimiento bruto\n\n\n\n\n                                                             Congruencia en                    Calidad\n                                                            el ajuste de metas\n\n\n\n\n                                                                              Nivel de logro\n                                                                           de cada indicador\n\n\n\n\n                                                                Resultado                       Gestión                  Promedio del nivel del logro\n                          Promedio del nivel del logro\n                         de los indicadores de Propósito                                                 30%         de los indicadores de Componentes\n                                                              70%\n\n\n\n\n                                                                        Cumplimiento efectivo\n                                                                        de metas por programa\n\n\n\n\nFuente: elaborado por el CONEVAL.\n\n13\n     Para consultar la metodología completa del CEM, ver: https://www.coneval.org.mx/coordinacion/Documents/monitoreo/informes/Informe_Cumplimiento_efectivo_de_me-\n     tas_2020.pdf\n\n\n\n\n                                                           Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social      41\n\nCon base en el CEM, los programas y acciones sociales se clasifican en cuatro niveles de cumpli-\nmiento: destacado, adecuado, bajo y deficiente. Cada nivel cuenta con un descriptor que permite\ninterpretar de manera general sus resultados.\n\n\nEs importante resaltar que el CEM captura el cumplimiento de las metas de los indicadores adopta-\ndos por los programas dentro de su Matriz de Indicadores y no considera indicadores externos a este\ninstrumento, por lo que los resultados obtenidos están limitados a lo que el programa refleje en su MIR\ny a las variaciones en su presupuesto.\n\n\nUniverso de análisis\n\nPara estimar el Cumplimiento Efectivo de Metas, como se describió en el apartado metodológico, se\nconsideran solamente los indicadores de nivel Propósito (resultados) y Componente (entrega de bie-\nnes o servicios) de los programas presupuestarios. En 2021,12 los programas prioritarios emplearon 124\nindicadores, tanto de Propósito como de Componente, 27 indicadores más que en 2020.\n\n\nLas proporciones entre ambos niveles de indicadores se mantuvieron relativamente similares en estos\naños, sin embargo, se incrementó la proporción de indicadores de resultados (Propósito) en dos pun-\ntos porcentuales, al pasar de un 44 % (43 indicadores) en 2020 a un 46 % (57 indicadores) en 2021. Por\nconsiguiente, se disminuyó la proporción, no así en términos absolutos, empleada para monitorear la\nentrega de sus bienes o servicios (Componente) al pasar de un 56 % (54 indicadores) en 2020 al 54 %\n(67 indicadores) en 2022.\n\n\nGráfica 10. Por un\nlado, programas como Pensión para el Bienestar de las Personas Adultas Mayores (S176) cuenta con\nuna calificación destacada en el CEM y tiene el mayor presupuesto dentro de los prioritarios, por otro\nlado, programas como Atención a la Salud y Medicamentos Gratuitos para la Población sin Seguridad\nSocial Laboral (U013) tiene el segundo mayor presupuesto, a pesar de tener un CEM deficiente.\n\n\nGráfica 11. 2021 y el año 2022, se\nobserva que la mayoría de los programas prioritarios tuvieron disminuciones en este concepto en\ntérminos reales, a excepción de los programas Fertilizantes (S292), con un incremento del 153%; Pen-\nsión para el Bienestar de las Personas Adultas Mayores (S176) con un aumento del 63% y Pensión para\nel Bienestar de las Personas con Discapacidad Permanente (S286). No obstante, solo el programa\nPensión para el Bienestar de las Personas Adultas Mayores (S176) tuvo un CEM destacado, mientras\nque los otros dos tuvieron un CEM bajo o deficiente. De igual forma, al calcular el coeficiente de\ncorrelación entre el cambio presupuestal y el CEM de los 16 programas prioritarios, este resultó en\n-0.07, lo que podría interpretarse como una relación prácticamente nula entre ambas variables, es\ndecir, que no hay una asociación entre la asignación presupuestaria y el cumplimiento de metas de\nlos programas prioritarios. En este sentido, se propone a los actores responsables de la asignación\npresupuestaria, tomar en cuenta el CEM como parte de los criterios utilizados en el proceso de asig-\nnación presupuestaria de los programas y acciones sociales.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                                                    Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social                                                  43\n\nCumplimiento Efectivo de Metas de los programas\nprioritarios 2020 vs 2021\n\nEl Cumplimiento Efectivo de Metas de los programas prioritarios es muy similar entre 2020 y 2021, pues-\nto que alrededor del 56 % de ellos tuvo un cumplimiento adecuado o destacado en ambos años. No\nobstante, se observan variaciones importantes al revisarlos individualmente.\n\n\nEntre las diferencias, se encontró que, de los cuatro programas que tuvieron un CEM destacado en\n2020, solo Beca Universal para Estudiantes de Educación Media Superior (S311) se mantuvo en esta\ncategoría. De este grupo llaman la atención los programas Sembrando Vida (S287) y el Programa de\nApoyo para el Bienestar de las Niñas y Niños, Hijos de Madres Trabajadoras (S174). El primero pasó\nde estar en un nivel destacado en 2020 a un nivel bajo en 2021; mientras que el Programa de Apoyo\npara el Bienestar de las Niñas y Niños, Hijos de Madres Trabajadoras (S174) pasó de un cumplimiento\ndestacado en 2020 a uno deficiente en 2021; lo anterior debido a que ambos tuvieron una valoración\nbaja en sus indicadores de Propósito, además de reportar un sobrecumplimiento15 en uno de sus in-\ndicadores de este nivel.16\n\n\nPor otro lado, hay cuatro programas que contaban con un nivel bajo o deficiente en su CEM y que\nen 2021 lograron ubicarse en niveles de cumplimiento de metas adecuados o destacados, entre ellos\nse distinguen los dos programas prioritarios coordinados por la Sedatu,17 que en 2020 tenían un CEM\ndeficiente y en 2022 lograron un CEM adecuado. Para revisar el CEM de los programas prioritarios en\n2020 y 2021 consultar el anexo 5.\n\n\nGráfica. 12.
